    slivers(cv, buf, logs, cam, light)
    # ---------- thin logs: explicit stroke glyphs""")
r=r.replace('''def hash_angle(s):''','''def clean_line(x0, y0, x1, y1):
    """a clean-run line: DDA along the major axis with a CONSTANT run length (the slope is snapped
    to 1:1, 2:1, 3:1, 4:1 or flat / vertical equivalents) -- Ferrari's narrow-form rule."""
    dx, dy = x1 - x0, y1 - y0
    n = int(max(abs(dx), abs(dy)))
    if n == 0:
        return np.array([int(round(x0))]), np.array([int(round(y0))])
    if abs(dx) >= abs(dy):
        ratio = abs(dx) / max(abs(dy), 1e-6)
        run = min([1, 2, 3, 4, 99], key=lambda q: abs(q - ratio)) if ratio < 6 else 99
        xs = np.arange(n + 1) * np.sign(dx) + round(x0)
        ys = round(y0) + (np.arange(n + 1) // run) * np.sign(dy) * (run < 99)
    else:
        ratio = abs(dy) / max(abs(dx), 1e-6)
        run = min([1, 2, 3, 4, 99], key=lambda q: abs(q - ratio)) if ratio < 6 else 99
        ys = np.arange(n + 1) * np.sign(dy) + round(y0)
        xs = round(x0) + (np.arange(n + 1) // run) * np.sign(dx) * (run < 99)
    return xs.astype(int), ys.astype(int)


def slivers(cv, buf, logs, cam, light):
    """splinter ends: each sliver a clean-run 1-px line following the grain out past the break,
    lit (7) on the sun side of the log or turned (4), with a dark partner pixel beneath it."""
    H, W = cv.step.shape
    for lg in logs:
        for (base, dirv, ln, e) in getattr(lg, 'spikes', []):
            tip = base + dirv * ln
            (bx, tx), (by, ty) = cam.project(np.stack([base, tip]))
            if max(abs(tx - bx), abs(ty - by)) < 2:
                continue
            xs, ys = clean_line(bx, by, tx, ty)
            n = (base - lg.ends_frames()[e][0])
            lit = (n @ light.L > -0.02) and not light.overcast
            for j, (x, y) in enumerate(zip(xs, ys)):
                if 0 <= x < W and 0 <= y < H:
                    if cv.mat[y, x] != WOOD or buf['obj'][y, x] < 0 or j > 0:
                        cv.mat[y, x] = WOOD
                        cv.step[y, x] = 7 if lit else 4
                        if y + 1 < H and buf['obj'][y + 1, x] < 0 and cv.mat[y + 1, x] != WOOD:
                            cv.step[y + 1, x] = min(cv.step[y + 1, x], 2)


def hash_angle(s):''')
open('render.py','w').write(r)

Bash: Snap grain dashes to clean runs
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood/src && python3 - <<'EOF'
m=open('marks.py').read()
m=m.replace("""def _curve(""","""def _snap_runs(xi, yi, own, seg=5):
    \"\"\"re-draw a projected dash as clean runs: vertices every ~seg px along it, each span a
    constant-run line (1:1, 2:1, 3:1, 4:1, flat) -- no zigzag diagonals.\"\"\"
    from render import clean_line
    if len(xi) < 2:
        return xi, yi
    keep = np.concatenate([[True], (np.abs(np.diff(xi)) + np.abs(np.diff(yi))) > 0])
    xi, yi = xi[keep], yi[keep]
    idx = list(range(0, len(xi), seg))
    if idx[-1] != len(xi) - 1:
        idx.append(len(xi) - 1)
    X, Y = [], []
    for a, b in zip(idx[:-1], idx[1:]):
        lx, ly = clean_line(xi[a], yi[a], xi[b], yi[b])
        X.append(lx[:-1] if b != idx[-1] else lx); Y.append(ly[:-1] if b != idx[-1] else ly)
    X = np.concatenate(X); Y = np.concatenate(Y)
    H, W = own.shape
    ok = (X >= 0) & (X < W) & (Y >= 0) & (Y < H)
    X, Y = X[ok], Y[ok]
    ok = own[Y, X]
    return X[ok], Y[ok]

cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood && rm stages/s1_lights_4x.png && cat >> LESSONS.md <<'EOF'

### Stage 1, revisions after critic rounds 3–4

- **Sawn end:** no growth rings below 20 px radius. The rings drew as a spiral target, the
  cartoon-log icon. Weathered end grain at this scale is a flat, lighter face, a softened darker
  rim and one to three radial checks from the heart.
- **Splinter end:** `render.slivers` draws each sliver as a *clean-run* 1-px line
  (`render.clean_line` snaps the slope to 1:1, 2:1, 3:1, 4:1 or flat) following the grain past the
  jagged break, lit (7) or turned (4), with a dark partner pixel under it. It replaces the pixel
  litter.
- **The back-lit rim was a dashed line.** This was shadow acne: the sun ray from a grazing
  surface point hit its own log. A log now never casts onto itself (its own form shadow belongs to
  the class rule), and the rim tests the neighbour toward the sun along x, y and the diagonal, so
  it is one continuous run that breaks only where the form turns away.

---

## Stage 2: the wood's material

![compare](stages/s2_compare_5x.png)
![material](stages/s2_material_4x.png)
![detail](stages/s2_detail_8x.png)
![bark](stages/s2_bark_5x.png)

**What the photos show** (Stångehuvud log, Driftwood on Gravel bar, the braided bar):

- Silvered wood is smooth and pale. Its grain is long, gently waving dark lines along the axis,
  broken into dashes, a few pixels apart; they swell round knots and converge where the wood
  narrows.
- A few long *checks* are the darkest lines, often with a lit upper lip.
- *Knots* are the single most identifying feature: a dark eye with the grain flowing round it,
  and the outline bulging over it.
- *Fresh bark* is the inverse: dark, crossed by a braided network of lighter ridges. Ferrari's
  snow-forest trunks are exactly this: lit ridge lines interwoven along a dark trunk.

**How it is painted** (`src/marks.py`). Every mark is a curve θ(s) on the 3-D surface, sampled at
half-pixel spacing, projected, hidden where the surface faces away or is covered, and redrawn as
clean runs: a vertex every 5 px, each span a constant-run line (`_snap_runs`). Without the
snapping, grain on a diagonal log was zigzag litter.

- *Grain* is one value step darker than the pixel's own class. Its dashes wave (0.06–0.18 rad,
  a wavelength of 2–6 diameters), bend round knots and taper: the end pixels of each dash are a
  half step.
- **The form turns by mark density, not by checker.** With marks on, logs at least 7 px across
  use pure classes (no 50% checker), and the chance that a grain dash is drawn depends on the class
  under it: light 0.5, halftone 0.95, core 0.85. Ferrari's lit band stays clean in his grey trunk
  in the same way. Laid over the checker, the grain turned into dotted-line noise.
- *Checks* wander (0.05–0.1 rad), open to 2 px in their middle third on the anti-sun side, close
  to a half step at both ends, stop partway, and carry a 1-px lighter lip on the sun side where
  lit. My first check was a ruled full-length staircase (critic, round 4).
- *Knots* are now geometry (`geo.Log.knots`: a small sphere smooth-unioned into the surface), so
  the outline bulges. The mark is a dark eye (step 1) in a darker ring with a lit lip on the sun
  side, and the grain curves round it.
- *Protection:* marks never overwrite the outline row, so the silhouette stays one clean run.
- *Fresh bark* uses the same machinery on a darker ramp: lines 2 steps *lighter*, wobbling
  0.25–0.45 rad at a wavelength of 4–8 radii, so they cross and braid.

**Honest comparison.** At 4–5× the logs read as weathered grey wood with knots, which the smooth
stage-1 tube never did. Against the photo, the real wood is more gnarled: its silhouette is lumpier
and its hollows deeper. Against Ferrari's grey trunk, his marks are denser and more clustered, and
his dashes hold a strict 1-px height, which mine now also do. The 8× close-up (36 px diameter) is
the weakest: the classes are wide flat plateaus, and at that size Ferrari would carry more classes
and more structure. My bark is recognisably his braided-ridge language in grey. It is not yet his
richness of voids and cross-ties.

            self.knot_prims.append((p0 + n * r0 * 0.78, r0 * 0.36))
        # stub primitives
        self.stub_prims = []
        for (sf, th, ln, rf, tilt) in self.stubs:
            p0, ax, r0, frame = self.frame_at(sf * self.L)
            e1, e2 = frame
            dirv = np.cos(th) * e2 + np.sin(th) * e1        # e2 ~ up

            b = p0 + dirv * (r0 + ln)
            self.stub_prims.append((a, b, r0 * rf, r0 * rf * 0.6))

    def frame_at(self, s):
        i = int(np.clip(np.searchsorted(self.cum, s) - 1, 0, len(self.seglen) - 1))
        f = (s - self.cum[i]) / self.seglen[i]
        p = self.pts[i] * (1 - f) + self.pts[i + 1] * f
        r = self.rad[i] * (1 - f) + self.rad[i + 1] * f
        ax = (self.pts[i + 1] - self.pts[i]) / self.seglen[i]
        up = np.array([0, 0, 1.0])
            d = np.maximum(d, dc)
        return d, parts

    def sdf(self, p, with_parts=False):
        d = self.sdf_body(p)
        d, parts = self.cut(p, d)
        for (c, rk) in self.knot_prims:
            dk = np.linalg.norm(p - c, axis=-1) - rk
            d = smin(d, dk, rk * 0.8)
        for (a, b, r1, r2) in self.stub_prims:
            ds = sd_round_cone(p, a, b, r1, r2)
            dnew = smin(d, ds, r1 * 0.6)
            parts = np.where(ds < d, 3, parts)
            d = dnew
        if self.rootwad is not None:
            rw = self.rootwad
            c, out = self.ends_frames()[rw.get('end', 0)]
            r0 = self.rad[0 if rw.get('end', 0) == 0 else -1]
            up = np.array([0, 0, 1.0])
            e1 = norm(np.cross(up, out)); e2 = np.cross(out, e1)
            cc = c + out * rw['depth'] * 0.5 + np.array([0, 0, rw.get('lift', 0.0)])
            axes = np.stack([out, e1, e2])
            R = np.array([rw['depth'], rw['r'], rw['r'] * rw.get('squash', 0.9)])
            de = sd_ellipsoid(p, cc, R, axes)
            parts = np.where(de < d, 4, parts)
            d = smin(d, de, r0 * 0.8)
        if with_parts:
            return d, parts
        return d

    def bound(self):
        lo = self.pts.min(0) - self.rad.max() * 4
        hi = self.pts.max(0) + self.rad.max() * 4
        for (a, b, r1, r2) in self.stub_prims:
            lo = np.minimum(lo, np.minimum(a, b) - r1); hi = np.maximum(hi, np.maximum(a, b) + r1)
        if self.rootwad is not None:
            R = self.rootwad['r'] * 1.2 + self.rootwad['depth']
            lo -= R; hi += R
        c = 0.5 * (lo + hi)
        return c, np.linalg.norm(hi - lo) * 0.5

    def _make_roots(self, rng):
        """a root wad: the flared butt splits into n thick roots that leave the rim radially,
        tilted forward past the end, then sweep back; some are broken short.  Thin rootlets
        fringe the tips.  Roots that would dive into the gravel are kept (the wad rests on them)."""
        rw = self.rootwad
        e = rw.get('end', 0)
        c, out = self.ends_frames()[e]

        e1 = norm(np.cross(up, out)); e2 = np.cross(out, e1)
        n = rw.get('n', 9)
        spread = rw.get('spread', 1.0)
        base_phi = rng.uniform(0, 2 * np.pi)
        for i in range(n):
            phi = base_phi + (i + rng.uniform(-0.3, 0.3)) / n * 2 * np.pi
            u = np.cos(phi) * e2 + np.sin(phi) * e1
            a = c - out * r0 * 0.35 + u * r0 * 0.55
            L1 = r0 * rng.uniform(0.9, 1.9) * spread
            broken = rng.random() < 0.35
            if broken:
                L1 *= 0.45
            d1 = norm(u + out * rng.uniform(0.2, 0.6) + rng.normal(0, 0.15, 3))
            ra = r0 * rng.uniform(0.22, 0.4)
            b = a + d1 * L1
            rb = ra * rng.uniform(0.5, 0.7)
            self.limbs.append((a, b, ra, rb, 'root'))
            if not broken:
                d2 = norm(d1 - out * rng.uniform(0.3, 0.9) + rng.normal(0, 0.2, 3))
                L2 = L1 * rng.uniform(0.4, 0.9)
                cpt = b + d2 * L2
                self.limbs.append((b, cpt, rb, rb * 0.45, 'root'))
                # rootlets from the tip

                    pts = [cpt, cpt + d3 * r0 * rng.uniform(0.3, 0.8)]
                    pts.append(pts[-1] + norm(d3 + rng.normal(0, 0.5, 3)) * r0 * rng.uniform(0.2, 0.6))
                    self.rootlets.append(np.array(pts))
            else:
                # a broken root ends in a stub of torn fibres
                self.rootlets.append(np.array([b, b + d1 * r0 * 0.25]))

s=open('geo.py').read()
a=s.index("    def local(self, p):"); b=s.index("    def sdf_body(self, p):")
s=s[:a]+'''    def local(self, p):
        """(s, theta, rho, surface-dist) of points p relative to the nearest primitive (main
        segments, then limbs).  Also sets self._T (axis tangent) and self._R (local radius);
        s = -1 on limbs."""
        shp = p.shape[:-1]
        best = np.full(shp, np.inf)
        S = np.zeros(shp); TH = np.zeros(shp); RR = np.zeros(shp)
        TT = np.zeros(p.shape); RL = np.ones(shp)
        up = np.array([0, 0, 1.0])
        segs = [(self.pts[i], self.pts[i + 1], self.rad[i], self.rad[i + 1], self.cum[i], True)
                for i in range(len(self.seglen))]
        segs += [(a, b, r1, r2, -1.0, False) for (a, b, r1, r2, kind) in self.limbs]
        for (a, b, r1, r2, s0, main) in segs:
            ln = np.linalg.norm(b - a)
            ax = (b - a) / ln
            tr = (p - a) @ ax
            t = np.clip(tr, 0, ln)
            c = a + t[..., None] * ax
            v = p - c
            dax = np.linalg.norm(v, axis=-1)
            rloc = r1 + (r2 - r1) * t / ln
            dsurf = dax - rloc
            if not main:
                dsurf = dsurf + 1e-4        # ties go to the trunk
            m = dsurf < best
            e1 = norm(np.cross(up, ax)); e2 = np.cross(ax, e1)
            th = np.arctan2(v @ e1, v @ e2)
            best = np.where(m, dsurf, best)
            S = np.where(m, s0 + tr if main else -1.0, S)
            TH = np.where(m, th, TH)
            RR = np.where(m, dax / rloc, RR)
            TT = np.where(m[..., None], ax, TT)
            RL = np.where(m, rloc, RL)
        self._T = TT; self._R = RL
        return S, TH, RR, best

    return lg""")

"""Stage 3: branch stubs and root wads."""
import sys, os, time
sys.path.insert(0, os.path.dirname(__file__))
import numpy as np
import geo, render
from paint import Light
from util import board

ROOT = os.path.expanduser('~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood')


def cell(az=300, el=40, W=140, H=80, scale=45, pitch=32, seed=3, yaw=15, L=3.0, r=0.2, rootwad=True,
         n_stubs=3, flat=True, look=(0, 0, 0.2), marks=True, overcast=False, ends=('worn', 'snapped'), **kw):
    cam = geo.Camera(W, H, scale, pitch=pitch, look=look)
    rw = dict(n=9, spread=1.0, end=0) if rootwad else None
    lg = geo.make_log(L=L, r=r, yaw=yaw, seed=seed, ends=ends, rootwad=rw, n_stubs=n_stubs, **kw)
    li = Light(cam, az=az, el=el, overcast=overcast)
    cv, buf, sh = render.render([lg], cam, li, seed=seed, stone_m=0.06, opts=dict(gravel=not flat, marks=marks))
    return render.to_rgb(cv, render.grey_ramps())


if __name__ == '__main__':
    t = time.time()
    P = [(f'seed {s}', cell(seed=s)) for s in (3, 5)]
    board(P, scale=4, cols=1).save(f'{ROOT}/scratch/s3_try.png')
    print(time.time() - t)

    """thin roots (< ~1.5 px): each a clean-run 1-px stroke from the root tip outward.  Value by
    which way the strand faces: up-facing strands lit (6), others core (2); a stroke over the
    ground gets a dark partner pixel beneath it (Ferrari's twigs: stroke + rim, never a smear).
    Hidden where the log's own surface is in front."""
    H, W = cv.step.shape
    V = -cam.f
    for k, lg in enumerate(logs):
        for pl in getattr(lg, 'rootlets', []):
            xs_, ys_ = cam.project(pl)
            tpts = (pl - cam.look) @ cam.f + 60.0
            for i in range(len(pl) - 1):
                xs, ys = clean_line(xs_[i], ys_[i], xs_[i + 1], ys_[i + 1])
                n = len(xs)
                seg = pl[i + 1] - pl[i]
                facing_up = (np.cross(seg, np.cross([0, 0, 1.0], seg))[2] > 0)
                lit = not light.overcast
                for j, (x, y) in enumerate(zip(xs, ys)):
                    if not (0 <= x < W and 0 <= y < H):
                        continue
                    tt = tpts[i] + (tpts[i + 1] - tpts[i]) * j / max(n - 1, 1)
                    if buf['obj'][y, x] >= 0 and buf['t'][y, x] < tt - 0.02:
                        continue
                    cv.mat[y, x] = WOOD
                    cv.step[y, x] = 6 if (lit and j % 7 != 3) else 2
                    if y + 1 < H and buf['obj'][y + 1, x] < 0 and cv.mat[y + 1, x] != WOOD:
                        cv.step[y + 1, x] = min(cv.step[y + 1, x], 2)


def slivers(cv, buf, logs, cam, light):''')

Bash: Root wad as dark disc mass with fringe

s=open('geo.py').read()
# replace _make_roots body
a=s.index("    def _make_roots(self, rng):"); b=s.index("    def frame_at(self, s):")
s=s[:a]+'''    def _make_roots(self, rng):
        """a root wad (photo: Driftwood log on sand; braided bar): a MASS -- a flattened, rough
        disc of tangled roots and trapped grit at the butt, much darker than the silvered wood --
        with a few thick silvered roots breaking out of it radially (strokes/limbs) and a fringe of
        thin rootlets round its rim."""
        rw = self.rootwad
        e = rw.get('end', 0)
        c, out = self.ends_frames()[e]
        r0 = self.rad[0 if e == 0 else -1]
        up = np.array([0, 0, 1.0])
        e1 = norm(np.cross(up, out)); e2 = np.cross(out, e1)
        rd = r0 * rw.get('disc', 2.3)
        self.disc = (c + out * r0 * 0.15, rd, rd * rw.get('thick', 0.45), out, e1, e2)
        # thick roots: radial, from inside the disc out past its rim, then sweeping back
        n = rw.get('n', 6)
        base_phi = rng.uniform(0, 2 * np.pi)
        for i in range(n):
            phi = base_phi + (i + rng.uniform(-0.3, 0.3)) / n * 2 * np.pi
            u = np.cos(phi) * e2 + np.sin(phi) * e1
            if u[2] < -0.5:
                continue                     # worn off underneath
            a = c + out * r0 * 0.1 + u * rd * 0.45
            L1 = rd * rng.uniform(0.6, 1.1)
            d1 = norm(u + out * rng.uniform(-0.1, 0.25) + rng.normal(0, 0.12, 3))
            ra = r0 * rng.uniform(0.18, 0.32)
            b = a + d1 * L1
            self.limbs.append((a, b, ra, ra * 0.6, 'root'))
            if rng.random() < 0.6:
                d2 = norm(d1 - out * rng.uniform(0.2, 0.7) + rng.normal(0, 0.25, 3))
                cpt = b + d2 * L1 * rng.uniform(0.3, 0.6)
                self.limbs.append((b, cpt, ra * 0.6, ra * 0.3, 'root'))
                self.rootlets.append(np.array([cpt, cpt + norm(d2 + rng.normal(0, 0.4, 3)) * r0 * 0.5]))
        # the fringe: rootlets round the disc rim, radiating outward, 1-2 segments
        nf = rw.get('fringe', 28)
        for i in range(nf):
            phi = rng.uniform(0, 2 * np.pi)
            u = np.cos(phi) * e2 + np.sin(phi) * e1
            if u[2] < -0.4:
                continue
            a = self.disc[0] + u * rd * rng.uniform(0.85, 0.98) + out * rng.uniform(-0.3, 0.3) * self.disc[2]
            d = norm(u + rng.normal(0, 0.35, 3))
            p1 = a + d * rd * rng.uniform(0.12, 0.35)
            pts = [a, p1]
            if rng.random() < 0.5:
                pts.append(p1 + norm(d + rng.normal(0, 0.6, 3)) * rd * rng.uniform(0.08, 0.2))
            self.rootlets.append(np.array(pts))

'''+s[b:]

cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood/src && python3 - <<'EOF'
r=open('render.py').read()
r=r.replace("from paint import (WOOD, GRAVEL, RAW, BARK, Light, gravel_field, hash2, BAYER2)","from paint import (WOOD, GRAVEL, RAW, BARK, Light, gravel_field, hash2, BAYER2)\nROOTM = 5")

    ROOTM:  [12, 26, 42, 58, 76, 96, 118, 142],

    rootwad_mass(cv, buf, logs, cam, light, sh, seed)
    # ---------- marks: grain, checks, knots (stage 2)""")
r=r.replace('''def rootlets(cv, buf, logs, cam, light):''','''def rootwad_mass(cv, buf, logs, cam, light, sh, seed=0):
    """trigger: the disc of a root wad.  recipe (a textured MASS, SYNTHESIS: mass outlines are
    fringes, the form turns through mark density):
      * its own dark ramp; three form steps by N.L (lit 4, turned 3, shadow 2), cast shadow 1-2;
      * tangled roots: short curved 1-px strokes, 3-7 px, in random directions biased round the
        disc (tangential), two steps LIGHTER than the body -- dense on the lit side, sparse in
        shadow (density carries the turn);
      * voids: 1-2 px holes at step 0 between the strokes;
      * the fringe (rootlets) is drawn later as strokes breaking the outline."""
    H, W = cv.step.shape
    obj = buf['obj']; part = buf['part']
    ndl = (buf['N'] * light.L).sum(-1)
    for k, lg in enumerate(logs):
        m = (obj == k) & (part == 4)
        if not m.any():
            continue
        rng = np.random.default_rng(lg.seed * 13 + seed)
        base = np.where(ndl > 0.45, 4, np.where(ndl > 0.1, 3, 2))
        if light.overcast:
            base = np.where(buf['N'][..., 2] > 0.2, 4, 3)
        base = np.where(sh, np.minimum(base, 2), base)
        cv.mat[m] = ROOTM
        cv.step[m] = base[m]
        ys, xs = np.nonzero(m)
        cy, cx = ys.mean(), xs.mean()
        n = int(m.sum() / 5)
        for _ in range(n):
            j = rng.integers(len(ys)); y0, x0 = ys[j], xs[j]
            lit_here = base[y0, x0]
            if rng.random() > (0.9 if lit_here >= 4 else 0.55 if lit_here == 3 else 0.25):
                continue
            ang = np.arctan2(y0 - cy, x0 - cx) + np.pi / 2 + rng.normal(0, 0.7)
            L = rng.integers(3, 8)
            xl, yl = clean_line(x0, y0, x0 + np.cos(ang) * L, y0 + np.sin(ang) * L)
            for x, y in zip(xl, yl):
                if 0 <= x < W and 0 <= y < H and m[y, x]:
                    cv.step[y, x] = min(base[y, x] + 2, 7)
        # voids
        for _ in range(int(m.sum() / 22)):
            j = rng.integers(len(ys)); y0, x0 = ys[j], xs[j]
            for dy, dx in ((0, 0), (0, 1)) if rng.random() < 0.5 else ((0, 0),):
                y, x = y0 + dy, x0 + dx
                if 0 <= x < W and 0 <= y < H and m[y, x]:
                    cv.step[y, x] = 0


def rootlets(cv, buf, logs, cam, light):''')
open('render.py','w').write(r)

Bash: Clumped root wad mass, fringe off-mass
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood/src && python3 - <<'EOF'
r=open('render.py').read()
a=r.index("def rootwad_mass("); b=r.index("def rootlets(")
r=r[:a]+'''def rootwad_mass(cv, buf, logs, cam, light, sh, seed=0):
    """trigger: the disc of a root wad.  recipe -- a knobbly MASS (the way Ferrari clusters moss
    and foliage: clumps with a lit side and a dark side, crevices between, the turn carried by
    how much of each clump is lit):
      * its own dark ramp; the mass steps by N.L (lit 4, turned 3, shadow 2), cast shadow <= 2;
      * clumps: screen-space cells ~3.5 px across (root knuckles and tangles).  The sun side of
        each clump is +1, its anti-sun side -1, the crevice between clumps -1, and where three
        clumps meet a void (0);
      * the fringe (rootlets) is drawn separately and only OFF the mass, breaking its outline."""
    H, W = cv.step.shape
    obj = buf['obj']; part = buf['part']
    ndl = (buf['N'] * light.L).sum(-1)
    lx = light.L @ cam.r; ly = -(light.L @ cam.u)
    ln = np.hypot(lx, ly) + 1e-6
    sx, sy = (lx / ln, ly / ln) if not light.overcast else (0.0, -1.0)
    for k, lg in enumerate(logs):
        m = (obj == k) & (part == 4)
        if not m.any():
            continue
        rng = np.random.default_rng(lg.seed * 13 + seed)
        base = np.where(ndl > 0.45, 4, np.where(ndl > 0.1, 3, 2))
        if light.overcast:
            base = np.where(buf['N'][..., 2] > 0.2, 4, 3)
        base = np.where(sh, np.minimum(base, 2), base)
        ys, xs = np.nonzero(m)
        y0, y1, x0, x1 = ys.min(), ys.max() + 1, xs.min(), xs.max() + 1
        cell = max(2.5, min(4.5, 0.12 * np.sqrt(m.sum())))
        n = int((y1 - y0) * (x1 - x0) / cell ** 2) + 4
        cyx = np.stack([rng.uniform(y0 - 2, y1 + 2, n), rng.uniform(x0 - 2, x1 + 2, n)], 1)
        YY, XX = np.mgrid[y0:y1, x0:x1]
        dy = YY[..., None] - cyx[:, 0]; dx = XX[..., None] - cyx[:, 1]
        dist = np.hypot(dy, dx)
        o = np.argsort(dist, axis=-1)
        d1 = np.take_along_axis(dist, o[..., :1], -1)[..., 0]
        d2 = np.take_along_axis(dist, o[..., 1:2], -1)[..., 0]
        d3 = np.take_along_axis(dist, o[..., 2:3], -1)[..., 0]
        j = o[..., 0]
        ddy = np.take_along_axis(dy, j[..., None], -1)[..., 0]
        ddx = np.take_along_axis(dx, j[..., None], -1)[..., 0]
        side = (ddx * sx + ddy * sy) / (d1 + 1e-6)
        sub = base[y0:y1, x0:x1].copy()
        v = sub.copy()
        v = np.where((side > 0.35) & (d1 > 0.6), sub + 1, v)
        v = np.where((side < -0.45) & (d1 > 0.8), sub - 1, v)
        crev = (d2 - d1) < 0.9
        v = np.where(crev, sub - 1, v)
        void = crev & ((d3 - d1) < 1.6)
        v = np.where(void, 0, v)
        mm = m[y0:y1, x0:x1]
        cvs = cv.step[y0:y1, x0:x1]
        cvs[mm] = np.clip(v, 0, 7)[mm]
        cv.mat[m] = ROOTM


'''+r[b:]
# rootlets only off the mass for rootwad logs' fringe: skip pixels on the log itself
r=r.replace("""                    if buf['obj'][y, x] >= 0 and buf['t'][y, x] < tt - 0.02:
                        continue""","""                    if buf['obj'][y, x] >= 0 and (buf['t'][y, x] < tt - 0.02 or buf['part'][y, x] == 4):
                        continue""")
open('render.py','w').write(r)

r=open('render.py').read()

        cvs = cv.step[y0:y1, x0:x1]
        cvs[mm] = np.clip(v, 0, 7)[mm]
        cv.mat[m] = ROOTM

        cvs = cv.step[y0:y1, x0:x1]

        cv.mat[m] = ROOTM
        # the fringe: 1-px root ends breaking the outline where the mass meets the ground/sky,
        # radiating from the mass centre; clean runs 2-6 px, tapering to a half-step tip
        cy, cx = ys.mean(), xs.mean()
        free = obj < 0
        outl = m & (np.roll(free, 1, 0) | np.roll(free, -1, 0) | np.roll(free, 1, 1) | np.roll(free, -1, 1))
        oy, ox = np.nonzero(outl)
        for y_, x_ in zip(oy, ox):
            if rng.random() > 0.3:
                continue
            ang = np.arctan2(y_ - cy, x_ - cx) + rng.normal(0, 0.45)
            L = rng.integers(2, 7)
            xl, yl = clean_line(x_, y_, x_ + np.cos(ang) * L, y_ + np.sin(ang) * L)
            lit = (np.cos(ang) * sx + np.sin(ang) * sy) > -0.2 and not light.overcast
            for q, (x, y) in enumerate(zip(xl[1:], yl[1:])):
                if 0 <= x < W and 0 <= y < H and free[y, x] and cv.mat[y, x] != WOOD:
                    cv.mat[y, x] = ROOTM
                    tip = q >= len(xl) - 2
                    cv.step[y, x] = (5 if lit else 3) - (1 if tip else 0)
""")

def boards():
    from PIL import Image
    from util import fit_photo
    from s0a_pale_trunk import load
    ph = fit_photo(f'{ROOT}/refs/openverse_Driftwood_log_on_sand_in_wind_7550a86a.jpg', (200, 170, 720, 620), (104, 90))
    d = load('V02'); f = d['pal'][d['idx'][358:412, 250:340]]
    mp = np.array(Image.open(os.path.expanduser('~/work/pixelart-studies/shared/refs/ferrari_mossy_forest_pool.png')).convert('RGB'))[::2, ::2]
    roots = mp[330:420, 150:260]
    board([('photo: root wad + forks', ph), ('mine: root wad log', cell(seed=3, W=110, H=90, scale=34, look=(-0.6, 0, 0.3))),
           ('Ferrari V02: twigs as strokes', f), ('Ferrari forest pool: root flare', roots)], scale=4, cols=2,
          title='s3 stubs and root wads, 4x').save(f'{ROOT}/stages/s3_compare_4x.png')
    P = []
    for sd, az, el, lab in ((3, 300, 40, 'left-front'), (5, 60, 40, 'right-front'), (7, 180, 25, 'back'), (9, 0, 80, 'overcast')):
        P.append((f'seed {sd}, {lab}', cell(seed=sd, az=az, el=el, overcast=(lab == 'overcast'))))
    board(P, scale=4, cols=2, title='s3 root wads and stubs (flat ground), native x4').save(f'{ROOT}/stages/s3_rootwads_4x.png')
    P = []
    for sd in (2, 4, 6):
        P.append((f'stubs, seed {sd}', cell(seed=sd, rootwad=False, n_stubs=5, ends=('snapped', 'worn'))))
    board(P, scale=4, cols=1, title='s3 branch stubs, native x4').save(f'{ROOT}/stages/s3_stubs_4x.png')
    a = cell(seed=3)
    board([('8x: the root wad', a[0:60, 5:70])], scale=8).save(f'{ROOT}/stages/s3_detail_8x.png')


if __name__ == '__main__' and len(sys.argv) > 1:
    boards()

Bash: Append stage 3 to LESSONS
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood && cat >> LESSONS.md <<'EOF'

---

## Stage 3: branch stubs and root wads

![compare](stages/s3_compare_4x.png)
![root wads](stages/s3_rootwads_4x.png)
![stubs](stages/s3_stubs_4x.png)
![detail](stages/s3_detail_8x.png)

**A root wad is a mass; stubs and roots are strokes or small cylinders.** That is the brief's own
distinction, and the photo (*Driftwood log on sand*) shows it: the butt is a dark, rough, lumpy
disc of tangled roots and trapped grit, much darker than the silvered wood, with a few thick
silvered roots and forks breaking out of it.

- **Geometry** (`geo.Log._make_roots`): a flattened ellipsoid disc at the butt, 2.3× the trunk
  radius, its rim lobed by three sine terms so it isn't a shield. Four to six thick roots are
  round-cone limbs leaving it radially, some forked or swept back; the ones pointing down are
  worn away. The trunk flares 1.35× into the disc. The log *tilts* up onto its wad while the far
  end stays on the gravel: my first version lifted the whole log, which then floated.
- **Every limb gets its own axis.** `Log.local` finds the nearest primitive (trunk segment or
  limb) and returns its tangent, so stubs and roots carry the same five cylinder classes as the
  trunk. Before this, stubs took the trunk's axis and shaded wrongly.
- **The mass** (`render.rootwad_mass`) has its own dark ramp and three steps by N·L. Its texture is
  clumps, the way Ferrari clusters moss and foliage: screen-space cells about 3.5 px across. The
  sun side of each clump is +1, its anti-sun side −1, and a crevice pixel (−1) marks only the
  anti-sun boundary between clumps, with an occasional void (0) where three meet. The first
  version outlined every cell and looked like a pine cone or a cobbled street; a knuckle reads as
  light-top/dark-bottom, not as a cell.
- **The fringe.** Along the outline where the mass meets the ground, 30% of the edge pixels throw
  a 2–6 px clean-run root end outward from the mass centre, lit or dark by which way it faces,
  with a half-step tip. I first placed the fringe rootlets in 3-D on the disc rim. Nearly all of
  them ended up inside the lobed silhouette or hidden behind it, and the mass kept a clean
  "magazine cut-out" edge. A fringe is a property of the *screen* outline, so it has to be drawn
  there.
- **Stubs** (`make_log(n_stubs=…)`): broken branches, mostly on the upper half (the downward ones
  have broken off or are buried), tilted toward the tip as branches grow, worn to rounded points.
  Under back light the fringe and stubs glow, as V02's twigs do.

**Honest comparison.** At 4× the root-wad logs read at once, and the back-lit version, with its
glowing fringe, is the most Ferrari-like image so far. At 8× the mass is still mechanical: the
clumps are too even in size, and the wads all come out round, facing the same way at the same
size. The photo's wad is more irregular and less disc-like. Stubs are plausible but too alike.
EOF

Write: elements/driftwood/src/palette.py
"""palette.py -- the light, as palettes.  The index canvas (material, step) never changes with hour
or wetness; only these ramps do (Ferrari: the same index map serves morning and afternoon).

Each ramp has 8 steps in the value plan proved in greyscale:
    0 contact | 1 deep shadow | 2 core | 3 reflected rim || 4 halftone | 5 lit edge | 6 light | 7 face/glint
Rules (from the photos and Ferrari's trunks):

  * silvered wood is nearly neutral; the HUE comes from the light: lit steps take the sun's warmth,
    the shadow steps the sky's coolness -- a hue walk along one ramp, not added white/black;
  * the reflected rim (3) is the coolest step (sky and pale gravel bounce), like Ferrari's
    sky-blue trunk rim (V09 class 132), and sits between core and halftone in value;
  * wet wood is darker by about a third and turns warm brown (tannin); its light steps compress,
    but a specular glint (7) stays near white -- wet things are dark with sharp highlights;
  * gravel is a warm beige-grey held a step below the lit wood; its shadow steps go blue-violet;
  * the root-wad mass is a dark umber-grey (grit and old bark), the bark ramp a warm dark brown.
"""
import numpy as np

WOOD, GRAVEL, RAW, BARK, SAND, ROOTM = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5

# light colour and sky colour per hour (multipliers), and the black point / haze
HOURS = {
    'noon':     dict(sun=(1.00, 0.97, 0.90), sky=(0.86, 0.92, 1.06), lift=0.0),
    'morning':  dict(sun=(1.04, 0.95, 0.84), sky=(0.84, 0.90, 1.08), lift=0.02),
    'golden':   dict(sun=(1.12, 0.90, 0.68), sky=(0.80, 0.80, 1.05), lift=0.03),
    'overcast': dict(sun=(0.95, 0.96, 0.98), sky=(0.93, 0.96, 1.00), lift=0.04),
}

# base (neutral, noon-ish) ramps, value-first
BASE = {
    WOOD:   [(34, 32, 38), (66, 66, 76), (112, 110, 116), (124, 130, 142), (154, 150, 146),
             (180, 174, 164), (208, 200, 186), (232, 226, 210)],
    RAW:    [(34, 30, 30), (70, 64, 62), (120, 110, 100), (132, 128, 128), (168, 156, 140),
             (194, 182, 162), (220, 208, 184), (242, 234, 214)],
    GRAVEL: [(26, 24, 30), (54, 52, 62), (82, 80, 86), (104, 100, 100), (124, 118, 110),
             (142, 134, 122), (160, 152, 138), (178, 170, 154)],
    BARK:   [(20, 14, 12), (36, 26, 22), (54, 40, 32), (66, 52, 46), (84, 64, 48),
             (104, 80, 60), (124, 98, 74), (146, 118, 90)],
    ROOTM:  [(16, 13, 14), (32, 27, 28), (50, 43, 40), (64, 58, 58), (82, 72, 64),
             (102, 90, 78), (124, 110, 94), (148, 134, 114)],
}
# which steps are 'lit' (take the sun colour) vs 'shadow' (take the sky colour)
LIT = np.array([0, 0, 0, 0, 1, 1, 1, 1], float)
# wet wood: darker, warmer, glint kept
WET_WOOD = [(28, 22, 20), (50, 40, 36), (78, 64, 54), (84, 80, 84), (102, 84, 68),
            (120, 100, 80), (140, 118, 94), (230, 226, 216)]


def ramps(hour='noon', wet=0.0):
    h = HOURS[hour]
    sun = np.array(h['sun']); sky = np.array(h['sky'])
    out = {}
    for m, ramp in BASE.items():
        r = np.array(ramp, float)
        if m in (WOOD, RAW) and wet > 0:
            r = r * (1 - wet) + np.array(WET_WOOD, float) * wet
        mul = LIT[:, None] * sun + (1 - LIT[:, None]) * sky
        if hour == 'overcast':
            # no sun: compress the lit steps toward the halftone, everything takes the sky
            r[4:7] = r[4:7] * 0.92 + r[4] * 0.08
            mul = np.tile(sky, (8, 1))
        r = r * mul
        # the reflected-rim step is the coolest (sky + bounce)
        r[3] = r[3] * np.array([0.94, 0.99, 1.08])
        r = r + h['lift'] * 255 * (1 - r / 255)
        out[m] = [tuple(int(v) for v in np.clip(c, 0, 255)) for c in r]
    return out

    """trigger: a log with wet >= 0.5 (lying in or just out of the water).  recipe: switch its
    wood pixels to the wet ramp (index canvas unchanged: the palette carries the wetness), and
    add the specular glint -- pixels whose normal is within a few degrees of the half vector
    between sun and eye become step 7, a broken 1-px run along the top; wet things are dark with
    sharp highlights.  Overcast: the glint follows the bright sky overhead, broader and dimmer."""
    V = -cam.f
    Hv = light.L + V
    Hv = Hv / np.linalg.norm(Hv)
    if light.overcast:
        Hv = np.array([0, 0, 1.0]) + V; Hv = Hv / np.linalg.norm(Hv)
    for k, lg in enumerate(logs):
        if lg.wet < 0.5:
            continue
        m = (buf['obj'] == k) & (cv.mat == WOOD)
        cv.mat[m] = WETW
        spec = m & ((buf['N'] @ Hv) > (0.992 if not light.overcast else 0.97)) & ~sh
        cv.step[spec] = 7 if not light.overcast else 6


def rootwad_mass(''')

"""Stage 4: colour -- silver-grey wood under warm and cool light; wet versus dry."""
import sys, os, time
sys.path.insert(0, os.path.dirname(__file__))
import numpy as np
import geo, render, palette
from paint import Light
from util import board, fit_photo

ROOT = os.path.expanduser('~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood')
PHOTO = os.path.expanduser('~/work/pixelart-studies/transfer/refs/braided_driftwood_bar.jpg')
HOUR_LIGHT = {'noon': (300, 55), 'morning': (60, 25), 'golden': (250, 12), 'overcast': (0, 80)}


def cell(hour='noon', wet=0.0, W=140, H=80, scale=36, pitch=32, seed=3, yaw=15, L=3.0, r=0.2,
         rootwad=True, n_stubs=2, flat=False, look=(-0.2, 0, 0.2), az=None, el=None, ends=('worn', 'snapped')):
    cam = geo.Camera(W, H, scale, pitch=pitch, look=look)
    rw = dict(n=6, end=0) if rootwad else None
    lg = geo.make_log(L=L, r=r, yaw=yaw, seed=seed, ends=ends, rootwad=rw, n_stubs=n_stubs, wet=wet)
    a, e = HOUR_LIGHT[hour]
    li = Light(cam, az=az if az is not None else a, el=el if el is not None else e, overcast=(hour == 'overcast'))
    cv, buf, sh = render.render([lg], cam, li, seed=seed, stone_m=0.06, opts=dict(gravel=not flat))
    return render.to_rgb(cv, palette.ramps(hour, wet))


if __name__ == '__main__':
    t = time.time()
    P = []
    for hour in ('noon', 'morning', 'golden', 'overcast'):
        for wet in (0.0, 1.0):
            P.append((f'{hour}, {"wet" if wet else "dry"}', cell(hour, wet)))
    board(P, scale=4, cols=2, title='s4 colour: hour x wetness (native x4)').save(f'{ROOT}/stages/s4_hours_wet_4x.png')
    print(time.time() - t)

    """round-cone distance evaluated only where its bounding sphere could matter (lb < d+margin);
    elsewhere the bounding-sphere distance (a valid lower bound) stands in."""
    c = 0.5 * (a + b)
    R = 0.5 * np.linalg.norm(b - a) + max(r1, r2)
    lb = np.linalg.norm(p - c, axis=-1) - R
    m = lb < d + margin
    if m.any():
        out = lb.copy()
        out[m] = sd_round_cone(p[m], a, b, r1, r2)
        return out
    return lb


def smin(a, b, k):''')

cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood && cat >> LESSONS.md <<'EOF'

---

## Stage 4: colour

![hours](stages/s4_hours_wet_4x.png)
![compare](stages/s4_compare_6x.png)

The index canvas doesn't change with the hour or the wetness; only the ramps do
(`src/palette.py`). That is Ferrari's finding (one index map for morning and afternoon), and it
held here: every colour cell is the same (material, step) image as its greyscale twin.

- **Hue comes from the light.** Silvered wood is almost neutral. The lit steps (4–7) are
  multiplied by the sun colour (noon: faintly warm; golden: orange), and the shadow steps (0–3)
  by the sky colour (faintly blue; violet at golden hour). This is a hue walk along one ramp,
  not white or black added.
- **Restraint.** My first palette gave the reflected-rim step an extra cool push on top of the sky
  tint, and made the gravel's gap step cool too. The whole lower half of every log turned
  periwinkle, and the ground was sprinkled with blue. Ferrari's trunk core is a *warm* grey
  (131,123,123); only its outermost rim column leans blue. With the core warm-grey and the rim only
  mildly cool, the logs read as silver.
- **Arctic light is not cream.** The braided-bar photo's lit tops are pale grey, not the cream my
  first light steps gave. The wood's lights are now neutral-warm (204,200,190).
- **Wet wood** (`render.wet_logs` + the `WETW` ramp): about a third darker and turned warm brown
  (tannin), with the lit steps compressed, *plus* a specular glint. Pixels whose normal is within
  about 7° of the sun–eye half vector become the near-white step 7, a broken 1-px run along the
  top. Under overcast the glint follows the sky overhead, broader and dimmer. Wet things are dark
  with sharp highlights. A wet log without the glint just looked like a brown log.
- **Stones.** River shingle is mixed, so each gravel plate takes one of three ramps by its own
  hash: beige (most), blue-grey, rusty. When plates are under 3.5 px, the variety is kept rare
  (12% blue, 3% rust), because at that size every hue change is a noise pixel.

**Honest comparison.** Beside the photo the dry noon log has the right family: silver with cool
shadows and pale tops. The photo's wood is bluer and colder overall than mine (Svalbard light).
The golden-hour cells are the most Ferrari-like: warm rims on violet shadow, as in his dusk
scenes. The gravel is still louder than his grounds ever are: it's the main thing a blind viewer
would catch.
EOF

Write: elements/driftwood/src/pile.py
"""pile.py -- a drift pile: logs stranded together on a gravel bar, crossing and resting on each
other.

Observed (braided_driftwood_bar.jpg, Hoh gravel bar): stranded logs lie mostly ALONG the old flow
(parallel, within ~25 deg), a minority crossing at steep angles; big trunks form the base, smaller
pieces lie on and against them; root wads point upstream; the pile thins at its edges into lone
sticks.  Logs resting on others bridge between supports and sag toward the ground between them.

Placement (physics only as far as the painting needs): logs are placed largest first; each new
log's axis height is the UPPER CONVEX HULL of its support profile -- at every point along it,
the higher of the gravel and the tops of logs already beneath -- so it rests on its highest
supports and spans the gaps between them.
"""
import numpy as np
import geo


def top_of(lg, x, y):
    """height of log lg's upper surface above ground point (x, y), or -inf if not over it."""
    best = -np.inf
    for i in range(len(lg.pts) - 1):
        a, b = lg.pts[i], lg.pts[i + 1]
        ab = b[:2] - a[:2]; L2 = ab @ ab
        t = np.clip(((np.array([x, y]) - a[:2]) @ ab) / max(L2, 1e-9), 0, 1)
        c = a + (b - a) * t
        r = lg.rad[i] + (lg.rad[i + 1] - lg.rad[i]) * t
        d = np.hypot(x - c[0], y - c[1])
        if d < r:
            best = max(best, c[2] + np.sqrt(r * r - d * d))
    return best


def upper_hull(t, h):
    pts = sorted(zip(t, h))
    hull = []
    for p in pts:
        while len(hull) >= 2:
            (x1, y1), (x2, y2) = hull[-2], hull[-1]
            if (x2 - x1) * (p[1] - y1) - (y2 - y1) * (p[0] - x1) >= 0:
                hull.pop()
            else:
                break
        hull.append(p)
    hx, hy = zip(*hull)
    return np.interp(t, hx, hy)


def settle(lg, placed, sink=0.015):
    """lower/raise lg so it rests on the ground and on the logs already placed."""
    n = len(lg.pts)
    t = lg.cum / lg.L
    # support profile at the axis points (plus midpoints for a finer hull)
    tt = np.linspace(0, 1, 4 * n)
    P = np.array([np.interp(tt, t, lg.pts[:, k]) for k in range(3)]).T
    R = np.interp(tt, t, lg.rad)
    h = R - sink
    for q in placed:
        for j in range(len(tt)):
            tp = top_of(q, P[j, 0], P[j, 1])
            if tp > -np.inf:
                h[j] = max(h[j], tp + R[j] * 0.97)
    z = upper_hull(tt, h)
    pts = lg.pts.copy()
    pts[:, 2] = np.interp(t, tt, z)
    return geo.Log(pts, lg.rad, ends=lg.ends, stubs=lg.stubs, rootwad=lg.rootwad, seed=lg.seed,
                   kind=lg.kind, wet=lg.wet, knots=lg.knots)


def make_pile(seed=0, n=9, extent=(5.0, 2.5), flow_yaw=10.0, centre=(0, 0), r_range=(0.08, 0.26),
              rootwads=0.25, stubs=True, wet_frac=0.0, crossing=0.3):
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    specs = []
    for i in range(n):
        r = np.exp(rng.uniform(np.log(r_range[0]), np.log(r_range[1])))
        L = r * rng.uniform(10, 22)
        yaw = flow_yaw + (rng.normal(0, 12) if rng.random() > crossing else rng.uniform(35, 90) * rng.choice([-1, 1]))
        cx = centre[0] + rng.normal(0, extent[0] * 0.3)
        cy = centre[1] + rng.normal(0, extent[1] * 0.3)
        specs.append((r, L, yaw, cx, cy))
    specs.sort(key=lambda s: -s[0])          # largest first: they form the base
    placed = []
    for i, (r, L, yaw, cx, cy) in enumerate(specs):
        has_rw = rng.random() < rootwads and r > r_range[0] * 1.6
        ends = (('worn' if has_rw else rng.choice(['worn', 'sawn', 'snapped'])),
                rng.choice(['worn', 'snapped', 'splinter', 'worn']))
        lg = geo.make_log(L=L, r=r, yaw=yaw, centre=(cx, cy), seed=seed * 101 + i, ends=ends,
                          rootwad=dict(n=5, end=0) if has_rw else None,
                          n_stubs=int(rng.integers(0, 4)) if stubs else 0,
                          wet=1.0 if rng.random() < wet_frac else 0.0,
                          bend=rng.uniform(0.02, 0.08), taper=rng.uniform(0.6, 0.85))
        if not has_rw:
            lg = settle(lg, placed)
        else:
            # root wads keep their tilt; lift them if they sit on something
            lg2 = settle(lg, placed)
            if lg2.pts[:, 2].max() > lg.pts[:, 2].max() + 1e-3:
                lg = lg2
        placed.append(lg)
    return placed
Bash: Pile placement, log separation; first pile render
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood/src && python3 - <<'EOF'
r=open('render.py').read()
r=r.replace("""    if o['contact']:
        contact(cv, buf, logs, cam)""","""    if o['contact']:
        contact(cv, buf, logs, cam)
        separate_logs(cv, buf, logs, cam)""")
r=r.replace('''def to_rgb(cv, ramps):''','''def separate_logs(cv, buf, logs, cam):
    """neighbours separate by VALUE, not by outline (Ferrari c5):
      * where one log rests on another, the lower log's pixels just under the upper log's
        silhouette and within ~2 px of it in 3-D get the contact step (1) -- the crack between;
      * where a log passes in front of another, the rear log's pixels along the occluding edge
        drop two steps if the two tones would otherwise be within a step of each other."""
    obj = buf['obj']; t = buf['t']; P = buf['P']
    H, W = obj.shape
    wood = obj >= 0
    for dy, dx in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
        o2 = np.roll(np.roll(obj, -dy, 0), -dx, 1)       # neighbour value at (y+dy, x+dx)
        t2 = np.roll(np.roll(t, -dy, 0), -dx, 1)
        s2 = np.roll(np.roll(cv.step, -dy, 0), -dx, 1)
        # pixel p is a REAR log pixel next to a FRONT log pixel
        rear = wood & (o2 >= 0) & (o2 != obj) & (t2 < t - 0.02)
        ys, xs = np.nonzero(rear)
        for y, x in zip(ys, xs):
            front = logs[o2[y, x]]
            close = front.sdf(P[y, x][None])[0] < 2.2 / cam.scale
            if close and dy == 1:
                cv.step[y, x] = min(cv.step[y, x], 1)          # resting contact, below
            elif abs(int(s2[y, x]) - int(cv.step[y, x])) <= 1:
                cv.step[y, x] = max(cv.step[y, x] - 2, 0)


def to_rgb(cv, ramps):''')
open('render.py','w').write(r)

cat > s5_pile.py <<'EOF'
"""Stage 5: a pile -- logs crossing and resting on each other, on gravel, at three distances."""
import sys, os, time
sys.path.insert(0, os.path.dirname(__file__))
import numpy as np
import geo, render, palette, pile
from paint import Light
from util import board, fit_photo

ROOT = os.path.expanduser('~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood')
PHOTO = os.path.expanduser('~/work/pixelart-studies/transfer/refs/braided_driftwood_bar.jpg')


def pile_cell(seed=1, scale=30, W=160, H=100, pitch=30, hour='noon', az=300, el=45, n=9, flat=False,
              look=(0, 0, 0.3), stone_m=0.06, grey=False, **kw):
    cam = geo.Camera(W, H, scale, pitch=pitch, look=look)
    logs = pile.make_pile(seed=seed, n=n, **kw)
    li = Light(cam, az=az, el=el, overcast=(hour == 'overcast'))
    cv, buf, sh = render.render(logs, cam, li, seed=seed, stone_m=stone_m, opts=dict(gravel=not flat))
    if grey:
        return render.to_rgb(cv, render.grey_ramps())
    return render.to_rgb(cv, palette.ramps(hour, 0.0))


if __name__ == '__main__':
    t = time.time()
    a = pile_cell(grey=True, flat=True)
    b = pile_cell()
    board([('grey, flat ground', a), ('colour, gravel', b)], scale=4, cols=1).save(f'{ROOT}/scratch/s5_try.png')
    print(time.time() - t)
EOF

Bash: Append stage 5 to LESSONS
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood && cat >> LESSONS.md <<'EOF'

---

## Stage 5: a pile

![grey](stages/s5_grey_3x.png)
![distance](stages/s5_distance_3x.png)
![compare](stages/s5_compare_4x.png)

**Placement** (`src/pile.py`). Stranded logs lie mostly *along* the old flow (within about 12°),
with a minority crossing at steep angles. Big trunks form the base and smaller pieces lie on and
against them. Logs are placed largest first, and each new log's axis height is the **upper convex
hull of its support profile**: at every point along it, the higher of the gravel and the tops of
the logs beneath. So it rests on its highest supports and spans the gaps between them. A root-wad
log keeps its tilt unless something lifts it. `field=True` strews the logs across the whole bar
(the braided-bar photo) instead of clumping them into a jam.

**Separation by value, not by outline** (`render.separate_logs`, after Ferrari's c5 boulders):

- where one log rests on another, the lower log's pixels just under the upper log's silhouette,
  within about 2 px in 3-D, take the contact step: the dark crack between them;
- where a log passes in front of another, the rear log's pixels along the occluding edge drop two
  steps, but only if the two tones would otherwise sit within a step of each other. Most edges
  already separate through the classes and the cast shadows of the logs above.
- a quarter of the logs are a less-bleached grey (`kind='grey'`, its own darker ramp), as in the
  photo, where not every log is silver. This variety of value helps the pile read far more than
  any outline does.

**With distance** (`s5_distance`): at 32 px/m every log carries classes and grain; at 14 px/m the
grain thins and the root wads become dark knots with a fringe; at 6 px/m the logs are `thin_logs`
strokes, a light top over a dark underside; at 3 px/m the pile is a short cluster of light dashes
over dark ones. The gravel's plate size is held at ≥1.6 px, so the ground stays a calm speckle.

**Honest comparison.** The greyscale piles on plain ground are the best images in this study: they
read immediately as a tangle of silvered logs, with root wads and stubs. The strewn-bar colour
cell has the photo's composition (pale logs in loose rows across the gravel) but less of its
value range. The photo mixes near-white sawn lumber with dark wet trunks, and my logs are too
alike in value. Where logs cross, the intersections are sometimes messy: stray white end-face
pixels and a grain line crossing the contact. His V19 rock pile separates every form from its
neighbour by a clear value step. Mine does that mostly, not always.
EOF
echo ok

Write: elements/driftwood/src/driftwood.py
"""driftwood.py -- the technique as one call, and the parameter sheets.

    paint(length=3.0, diameter=0.35, orientation=15, rootwad=False, weathering=1.0, wetness=0.0,
          light='left-front', hour='noon', camera='30', distance='near', seed=0, pile=0,
          ground='gravel', W=160, H=96) -> RGB uint8 array (native pixels)

  length, diameter   metres
  orientation        compass yaw of the log in degrees (0 = across the view, 90 = pointing away)
  rootwad            a root wad at the butt end
  weathering         0..1: grain gaps, checks and knots (0 = smooth, 1 = checked and knotted)
  wetness            0..1: >= 0.5 switches the log to the wet ramp with specular glints
  light              'left-front' | 'right-front' | 'back' | 'high' (sun azimuth/elevation)
  hour               'noon' | 'morning' | 'golden' | 'overcast' (the palette; overcast ignores light)
  camera             '30' (30 deg oblique) | 'low' (10 deg)
  distance           'near' (40 px/m) | 'mid' (16) | 'far' (6) | a number (px/m)
  pile               0 = one log; n > 0 = a pile of n logs around it (seed-driven)

    python3 driftwood.py sheets        # all sheets into ../sheets (4 worker processes)
"""
import sys, os, time
sys.path.insert(0, os.path.dirname(__file__))
import numpy as np
import geo, render, palette, pile as pilemod
from paint import Light
from util import board

ROOT = os.path.expanduser('~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood')
LIGHTS = {'left-front': (300, 40), 'right-front': (60, 40), 'back': (180, 22), 'high': (330, 68)}
HOUR_EL = {'golden': 10, 'morning': 22}
DIST = {'near': 40.0, 'mid': 16.0, 'far': 6.0}
PITCH = {'30': 30.0, 'low': 10.0}


def paint(length=3.0, diameter=0.35, orientation=15.0, rootwad=False, weathering=1.0, wetness=0.0,
          light='left-front', hour='noon', camera='30', distance='near', seed=0, pile=0,
          ground='gravel', W=160, H=96, grey=False):
    scale = DIST.get(distance, distance) if isinstance(distance, str) else float(distance)
    pitch = PITCH[camera]
    r = diameter / 2
    az, el = LIGHTS[light]
    if hour in HOUR_EL:
        el = min(el, HOUR_EL[hour])
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    rw = dict(n=6, end=0) if rootwad else None
    lg = geo.make_log(L=length, r=r, yaw=orientation, seed=seed, rootwad=rw,
                      ends=('worn' if rootwad else rng.choice(['sawn', 'worn', 'snapped'])),
                      n_stubs=int(round(3 * weathering)), wet=1.0 if wetness >= 0.5 else 0.0,
                      n_knots=int(round(3 * weathering)))
    lg.ends = (lg.ends if isinstance(lg.ends, tuple) else (lg.ends, 'snapped'))
    logs = [lg]
    if pile:
        extra = pilemod.make_pile(seed=seed + 1, n=pile, extent=(length * 1.6, length * 0.8),
                                  flow_yaw=orientation, r_range=(r * 0.4, r * 0.9))
        placed = [lg]
        for q in extra:
            placed.append(pilemod.settle(q, placed))
        logs = placed
    cz = float(np.mean([q.pts[:, 2].mean() for q in logs]))
    cx = float(np.mean(lg.pts[:, 0])); cy = float(np.mean(lg.pts[:, 1]))
    cam = geo.Camera(W, H, scale, pitch=pitch, look=(cx, cy, cz))
    li = Light(cam, az=az, el=el, overcast=(hour == 'overcast'))
    cv, buf, sh = render.render(logs, cam, li, seed=seed, stone_m=max(0.06, 1.6 / scale),
                                opts=dict(gravel=(ground == 'gravel'), weather=weathering))
    if grey:
        return render.to_rgb(cv, render.grey_ramps())
    return render.to_rgb(cv, palette.ramps(hour, 1.0 if wetness >= 0.5 else 0.0))


# ------------------------------------------------------------------ sheets
def _cell(args):
    label, kw = args
    os.nice(10) if hasattr(os, 'nice') else None
    t = time.time()
    img = paint(**kw)
    return label, img, time.time() - t


def sheet(name, cells, cols, title, scale=3):
    from multiprocessing import Pool
    with Pool(4) as p:
        res = p.map(_cell, cells)
    panels = [(lab, img) for lab, img, _ in res]
    board(panels, scale=scale, cols=cols, title=title).save(f'{ROOT}/sheets/{name}_{scale}x.png')
    board(panels, scale=1, cols=cols, title=None, fsz=9, label_h=12).save(f'{ROOT}/sheets/{name}_1x.png')
    print(name, 'cells', len(cells), 'mean s/cell', round(np.mean([r[2] for r in res]), 1))


SHEETS = {}


def _def(name):
    def deco(f):
        SHEETS[name] = f
        return f
    return deco


@_def('length_diameter')
def s_len_diam():
    cells = []
    for L in (1.5, 3.0, 5.0):
        for d in (0.15, 0.3, 0.55):
            cells.append((f'L {L} m, d {d} m', dict(length=L, diameter=d, seed=11, distance=28.0)))
    sheet('sheet_length_diameter', cells, 3, 'length (rows) x diameter (cols); 28 px/m, 30 deg, noon, left-front')


@_def('orientation_camera')
def s_orient_cam():
    cells = []
    for cam in ('30', 'low'):
        for o in (0, 30, 60, 90):
            cells.append((f'camera {cam}, yaw {o}', dict(orientation=o, camera=cam, seed=12, distance=30.0,
                                                          H=80 if cam == 'low' else 96)))
    sheet('sheet_orientation_camera', cells, 4, 'orientation (cols) x camera 30 deg / low 10 deg (rows)')


@_def('rootwad_weather_wet')
def s_rw():
    cells = []
    for rw in (False, True):
        for w, wt in ((0.2, 0.0), (1.0, 0.0), (0.2, 1.0), (1.0, 1.0)):
            cells.append((f'{"root wad" if rw else "no wad"}, weather {w}, {"wet" if wt else "dry"}',
                          dict(rootwad=rw, weathering=w, wetness=wt, seed=13, distance=30.0, diameter=0.4)))
    sheet('sheet_rootwad_weathering_wetness', cells, 4, 'root wad (rows) x weathering and wetness (cols)')


@_def('light_hour')
def s_light_hour():
    cells = []
    for hour in ('noon', 'morning', 'golden', 'overcast'):
        for lt in ('left-front', 'right-front', 'back', 'high'):
            cells.append((f'{hour}, {lt}', dict(light=lt, hour=hour, seed=14, distance=30.0, rootwad=True, diameter=0.4)))
    sheet('sheet_light_hour', cells, 4, 'hour (rows) x sun direction (cols)')


@_def('distance_seed')
def s_dist_seed():
    cells = []
    for dist in ('near', 'mid', 'far'):
        for sd in (21, 22, 23, 24):
            cells.append((f'{dist}, seed {sd}', dict(distance=dist, seed=sd, rootwad=(sd % 2 == 0), pile=6,
                                                     length=3.5, diameter=0.4)))
    sheet('sheet_distance_seed', cells, 4, 'distance (rows: 40, 16, 6 px/m) x seed (cols), with a 6-log pile')


if __name__ == '__main__':
    if len(sys.argv) > 1 and sys.argv[1] == 'sheets':
        names = sys.argv[2:] or list(SHEETS)
        for n in names:
            SHEETS[n]()
    else:
        from PIL import Image
        img = paint()
        Image.fromarray(img).resize((img.shape[1] * 4, img.shape[0] * 4), Image.NEAREST).save(f'{ROOT}/scratch/paint_default.png')

cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood/src && python3 - <<'EOF'

s=s.replace("""                      ends=('worn' if rootwad else rng.choice(['sawn', 'worn', 'snapped'])),""","""                      ends=('worn' if rootwad else str(rng.choice(['sawn', 'worn', 'snapped'])),
                            str(rng.choice(['snapped', 'worn', 'splinter']))),""")
s=s.replace("""    lg.ends = (lg.ends if isinstance(lg.ends, tuple) else (lg.ends, 'snapped'))\n""","")
open('driftwood.py','w').write(s)
EOF
time nice -n 10 python3 driftwood.py

## Round 5 — 2026-09-25 22:12 — `stages/s2_material_4x.png` (the other s2 images are unchanged since round 4)
Round-4 notes stand. One addition from `s2_material_4x.png`: **the knots are 2×2 dark squares** (seed 4, seed 11 heavy), which read as bullet holes. A knot on weathered wood is a lens-shaped lighter or darker boss, often with a dark centre, and *the grain streams around it*, bowing out on both sides. The deflected grain is what says "knot"; the dot alone doesn't. The white vertical cut ends (seed 7, seed 11) also still read as sawn lumber rather than driftwood.

# output:
## Round 10 — 2026-09-25 23:14 — stage 5 `stages/s5_compare_4x.png`, `s5_distance_3x.png`, `s5_grey_3x.png`
**Stage 5: REVISE.** (My read; your call.)

1. **Everything points the same way.** In "mine: a pile at 9 px/m" and "near, 32 px/m" every log lies within about 15° of horizontal, at similar lengths, so the pile reads as stacked lumber, or at 9 px/m as a spaceship glyph. Your own photo above it shows the real pile: logs at every angle, crossing one another, several with round *ends facing the camera* (big pale discs with dark rims, the most readable shapes in the photo), and short broken pieces mixed with long ones.
2. **No shadows between logs.** Where one log lies across another, the upper one throws a dark shadow band onto the lower one, and there's a black pocket where they meet. That's what separates the forms. The Ferrari V19 panel on your own sheet says it in its label: "forms separated by value." Your logs overlap with no value break.
3. **The stubs read as antennae** at 9 and 6 px/m. At that distance a stub is one or two pixels, or nothing.
4. **The root wads are still dark hairy balls** (mid, 14 px/m; round 8).
5. **The ground** is the same blue-tan static. At 3 px/m the pile is quieter than the ground around it.

## Round 9 — 2026-09-25 23:00 — `stages/s4_compare_6x.png`
One look at the new 6× comparison: next to the braided-bar photo, the photo's logs are pale silver-grey on *both* sides, with the shadow side only a little darker and cooler. Yours ("mine noon dry") is a cream top over a strong periwinkle underside. The shadow hue shift is about three times too strong. The wet version reads right. The ground is still louder than the log (round 8).

## Round 8 — 2026-09-25 22:48 — stage 3 `stages/s3_compare_4x.png`, `s3_rootwads_4x.png`, `s3_stubs_4x.png`, `s3_detail_8x.png`; stage 4 `s4_hours_wet_4x.png`
**Stage 3: REVISE. Stage 4: REVISE.** Branch stubs and the wet variant are real steps; the root wad is the weak point. (My read; your call. Note also that `s3_compare_4x`'s photo panel is mostly black; the photo crop didn't load.)

### Stage 3

1. **The root wad reads as a dark hairy ball stuck on the end:** a pinecone or a mop head. In your refs (`commons_Upturned_root_wad_*`, `openverse_Root_wads_*`, and above all `openverse_Driftwood_on_Gravel_bar_*`), a driftwood root wad is *the same silvered wood as the trunk*. The trunk flares continuously into a few thick twisting roots that radiate out and taper, with big dark voids between them where you see through. Yours has a dark, textured disc that the trunk simply butts into, with thin pale sticks poking out like whiskers. The flare, the voids, and the continuity of material are what make it a root wad.
2. **The 8× detail is speckle.** The wad interior (`s3_detail_8x`) is dark noise: no root forms, no light on individual roots. Each root is a tapering cylinder, and it should get the same lit top and dark underside as the trunk.
3. **Stubs look pegged in.** They're short pale rectangl

Bash: Silvered root fan, limb culling, shadow family jump
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood/src && python3 - <<'EOF'
s=open('geo.py').read()
s=s.replace('''        rw = self.rootwad
        e = rw.get('end', 0)
        c, out = self.ends_frames()[e]
        r0 = self.rad[0 if e == 0 else -1]
        up = np.array([0, 0, 1.0])
        e1 = norm(np.cross(up, out)); e2 = np.cross(out, e1)
        rd = r0 * rw.get('disc', 2.3)''','''        rw = self.rootwad
        e = rw.get('end', 0)
        c, out = self.ends_frames()[e]
        r0 = self.rad[0 if e == 0 else -1]
        up = np.array([0, 0, 1.0])
        e1 = norm(np.cross(up, out)); e2 = np.cross(out, e1)
        if rw.get('style', 'fan') == 'fan':
            return self._make_fan(rng, c, out, r0, e1, e2)
        rd = r0 * rw.get('disc', 2.3)''')
s=s.replace('''    def frame_at(self, s):''','''    def _make_fan(self, rng, c, out, r0, e1, e2):
        """the silvered root fan (critic r8; Upturned root wad, Driftwood on Gravel bar): the SAME
        weathered wood as the trunk, which flares continuously into 5-8 thick roots that radiate,
        twist and taper, with big voids between them where the gravel shows through.  Each root
        is 2-3 round-cone segments, so it carries the trunk's own five classes.  Roots that point
        into the gravel are worn off short; thin tips end in 1-px rootlets."""
        rw = self.rootwad
        n = rw.get('n', 7)
        base_phi = rng.uniform(0, 2 * np.pi)
        for i in range(n):
            phi = base_phi + (i + rng.uniform(-0.3, 0.3)) / n * 2 * np.pi
            u = np.cos(phi) * e2 + np.sin(phi) * e1
            down = u[2] < -0.35
            a = c - out * r0 * rng.uniform(0.2, 0.6) + u * r0 * 0.55
            ra = r0 * rng.uniform(0.3, 0.46)
            d = norm(u + out * rng.uniform(0.0, 0.35) + rng.normal(0, 0.1, 3))
            nseg = 1 if down else int(rng.integers(2, 4))
            L = r0 * rng.uniform(0.7, 1.2) * (0.5 if down else 1.0)
            r1 = ra
            for sgi in range(nseg):
                b = a + d * L
                r2 = r1 * rng.uniform(0.5, 0.7)
                self.limbs.append((a, b, r1, r2, 'root'))
                # twist: turn about the axis direction and sweep back toward the trunk
                d = norm(d + np.cross(out, d) * rng.normal(0, 0.45) - out * rng.uniform(0.1, 0.5)
                         + np.array([0, 0, -0.15]))
                a, r1, L = b, r2, L * rng.uniform(0.6, 0.9)
            if not down and r1 > r0 * 0.06:
                pts = [a, a + norm(d + rng.normal(0, 0.4, 3)) * r0 * rng.uniform(0.3, 0.7)]
                self.rootlets.append(np.array(pts))

    def frame_at(self, s):''')
s=s.replace("rad[j0] *= rootwad.get('flare', 1.35); rad[j1] *= 1 + (rootwad.get('flare', 1.35) - 1) * 0.3","fl = rootwad.get('flare', 1.6 if rootwad.get('style', 'fan') == 'fan' else 1.35)\n        rad[j0] *= fl; rad[j1] *= 1 + (fl - 1) * 0.35")
open('geo.py','w').write(s)

r=open('render.py').read()
r=r.replace("""    H, W = cam.H, cam.W
    buf = geo.trace(cam, logs)""","""    H, W = cam.H, cam.W
    # what is below a pixel does not exist: limbs thinner than ~0.6 px and rootlets on logs under
    # ~3 px radius are dropped (critic r10: stubs read as antennae at 6-9 px/m)
    for lg in logs:
        lg.limbs = [l for l in lg.limbs if l[2] * cam.scale >= 0.6]
        if lg.rad.mean() * cam.scale < 3:
            lg.rootlets = []
    buf = geo.trace(cam, logs)""")
r=r.replace("""    # ---------- end faces
    st = end_faces(st, buf, logs, light, sh, cam)""","""    # a cast shadow on wood is a FAMILY jump, not a class nudge: shadowed wood drops to the core
    # tone (2), or deep shadow (1) where it also turns from the sun -- the value break that
    # separates one log from the one lying across it (critic r10)
    if not light.overcast:
        ndl_c = (buf['N'] * light.L).sum(-1)
        st = np.where(wood & sh, np.where(ndl_c > 0.2, 2, 1), st)
    # ---------- end faces
    st = end_faces(st, buf, logs, light, sh, cam)""")
open('render.py','w').write(r)
EOF

+ **Honest comparison (of that first version).** At 4× the root-wad logs read at once, and the
+ back-lit version, with its glowing fringe, was the most Ferrari-like image at that point. At 8×
+ the mass was mechanical: the clumps were too even in size, and the wads all came out round,
+ facing the same way at the same size.
+ 
+ ### Stage 3, revision: the silvered root fan (critic round 8)
+ 
+ The critic's point, which the refs (*Upturned root wad*, *Root wads*) bear out: most root wads
+ stranded on a river bar are washed clean. They are **the same silvered wood as the trunk**, which
+ flares continuously into a few thick roots that radiate, twist and taper, with big voids between
+ them where the gravel shows through. My dark disc read as a pine cone or a mop head stuck on the
+ end. The *Driftwood log on sand* wad is the exception (grit and barnacles), not the rule.
+ 
+ - `geo.Log._make_fan` is now the default (`rootwad=dict(style='fan')`; `style='mass'` keeps the
+   dark disc). The trunk flares 1.4× and 8–9 roots leave the flare's rim radially. Each root is two
+   or three round-cone segments that twist about the trunk axis, sweep back and sag, tapering
+   0.5–0.7× per segment. Roots pointing into the gravel are worn short. Thin tips end in 1-px
+   rootlets. The butt itself is a snapped break between the roots.
+ - Because every root is a limb with its own axis, it carries the trunk's five classes: lit top,
+   core, reflected underside. The voids need no painting, since they are simply ground seen
+   between the roots.
+ - **Orientation matters for reading.** Seen from the side (log across the view), the fan is edge-on
+   and collapses to a few spikes. Seen from three-quarters with the butt toward the viewer, it reads
+   at once. The stage-3 boards now use that view; the sheets sweep orientation.
+ - Limbs thinner than about 0.6 px and rootlets on distant logs are dropped before tracing: what is
+   below a pixel does not exist. At 6–9 px/m the stubs had read as antennae (critic round 10).
+ 
+ ![root wads, revised](stages/s3_rootwads_4x.png)
+ 
+ **Honest comparison, now.** The fan reads as a root wad, silver like its trunk, and it is closer
+ to the photos than the dark disc was. It is still too regular: the roots are evenly spaced, and the
+ thick part of each root is a smooth round cone, where a real root is flattened and buttressed
+ where it leaves the trunk. The butt between the roots is a pale lump rather than a torn tangle.
Bash: Append revisions, stage 6, and honest summary
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood && cat >> LESSONS.md <<'EOF'

### Stages 4–5, revisions (critic rounds 8–10)

- **The shadow hue was three times too strong** (round 9). Beside the braided-bar photo, silvered
  logs are pale on *both* sides, the shadow side only a little darker and cooler. The sky
  multipliers are now a third of what they were (noon: 0.98, 0.99, 1.01), and the wood's
  core/rim/halftone steps sit closer together. The golden hour keeps a stronger violet, as
  Ferrari's dusks do.
- **The ground was louder than the logs** (rounds 3, 8, 10). The plates' faces now sit on one step
  with a few a step up, the gaps one step down, and the stone-type ramps are desaturated. The
  gravel is a quiet two-value speckle that the logs read against.
- **A cast shadow on wood is a family jump** (round 10): shadowed wood drops to the core tone, or
  to deep shadow where it also turns from the sun. The class rule alone only nudged it by one
  class. This is the value break that separates a log from the one lying across it.
- **The piles all pointed one way** (round 10). Now 35% of logs cross at any angle, 15% point at
  the camera (their ends face the viewer, the most readable shapes in the photo), and a third are
  short broken pieces. Sawn ends are rarer; worn ends are the commonest.
- *Bark* (round 6): the body now sits two steps down, and the ridges are three steps up. It is
  dark with bright braided ridges, as in Ferrari's snow-forest trunks, and it no longer reads as
  mid-grey marble. My first "voids" were random dark pixels, which read as noise. The voids are
  simply the dark body between ridges.
- *Checks* wander more (0.1–0.18 rad at a wavelength of 1.5–4 diameters), so they are no longer
  an equal-step staircase.

---

## Stage 6: the technique as code, and the sheets

`src/driftwood.py` is the one call:



paint(length=3.0, diameter=0.35, orientation=15, rootwad=False, weathering=1.0, wetness=0.0,
      light=&#x27;left-front&#x27;, hour=&#x27;noon&#x27;, camera=&#x27;30&#x27;, distance=&#x27;near&#x27;, seed=0, pile=0,
      ground=&#x27;gravel&#x27;, W=160, H=96)




- **light**: `left-front`, `right-front`, `back` or `high`.
- **hour**: `noon`, `morning`, `golden` or `overcast`.
- **camera**: `30` (30° oblique) or `low` (10°).
- **distance**: `near` (40 px/m), `mid` (16), `far` (6) or a number.
- **pile**: `n` adds n logs settled around the first.

`python3 src/driftwood.py sheets` writes every sheet, at 1× and 3×, using four worker processes.
`src/run_all.sh` regenerates every stage image and sheet in about nine minutes.

![length x diameter](sheets/sheet_length_diameter_3x.png)
![orientation x camera](sheets/sheet_orientation_camera_3x.png)
![root wad x weathering x wetness](sheets/sheet_rootwad_weathering_wetness_3x.png)
![hour x light](sheets/sheet_light_hour_3x.png)
![distance x seed](sheets/sheet_distance_seed_3x.png)

**A mechanical lesson worth passing on.** With numpy free to multithread, a cell took about 30 s
and the machine sat at 250% CPU. With `OMP_NUM_THREADS=1` and friends, it takes 1–6 s, since the
arrays are small and threads only fight. `run_all.sh` sets the variables, which also keeps the
brief's four-thread limit.

---

## Where it stands, honestly

**Closest to Ferrari:**

- the cylinder language (his five classes in his order, proven 89% on his own trunk);
- the contre-jour rims;
- the greyscale piles on plain ground;
- the golden-hour cells;
- the root fans seen from three-quarters.

**Not there yet, and exactly why:**

1. **Ground.** It is calmer now, but still a uniform speckle at one scale. Ferrari's grounds have
   big value masses (sweeps, patches of wet and dry, shadowed hollows) with texture inside them.
   Mine has texture and no masses. This is the first thing a blind viewer would catch.
2. **Big logs at 8×.** At a 30–40 px diameter the five classes become wide flat plateaus. Ferrari
   would carry more classes, or more structure at the plateau boundaries (grain bunching at the
   class seams). My grain dashes still read as blocks at 8×, not as strokes tapering into the
   tone.
3. **Stage 0B is not a blind match.** The language (one-class body, rim on everything, bold kinked
   twigs) matches, but the placement of individual twigs is a sketch.
4. **Pile intersections** sometimes carry stray end-face pixels or a grain line through the
   contact crack. The logs in a pile are still too alike in value compared with the photo's mix of
   near-white lumber and dark wet trunks.
5. **Root fans** are too regular: evenly spaced round roots, with no flattened buttresses.

**What would help** (for the coordinator): Ferrari has no driftwood and few lit fallen logs. A
second artist's pixel driftwood or beach logs at native resolution would be a closer target for
the lying-log passages. So would the GDC talk's slides on trunks, if they can be had. A stage-0
copy of his near-ground masses (V19's snowy ledges, V16's banks) aimed at the ground problem is
the next study I would make.

SubagentHandback:

I've handed in the driftwood study: all six stages and the Ferrari copies are done, but it doesn't match his work yet. The critic returned REVISE on every stage, and I've recorded which of its points I fixed and which gaps remain in LESSONS.md.

  • Ferrari copies (stage 0): his pale sunlit trunk turned out to be five tones in a fixed order across its width, held within about 55 grey levels. A lit 3-D cylinder built on that rule matches 89% of his pixels, and it became the core of the whole technique. The dusk fallen-tree copy (hand-drawn silhouette) matches his look: a near-black body, a 1-px lit rim on every edge, and bold kinked twigs. The placement of individual twigs is only a sketch, so it wouldn't pass a blind test.
  • Logs, material, colour: logs are bent, tapered and lumpy, with sawn, worn, snapped and splintered ends, grain and splits that follow the wood, knots the grain bends around, and simple stroke versions for small sizes. Hour and wetness only change the palette. After the critic's notes I made the shadows much less blue and replaced the dark root-wad disc with a fan of silvered roots.
  • Piles and sheets: logs are placed largest first and rest on each other. Piles now have varied angles, some ends toward the camera and some short broken pieces. Neighbouring logs are separated by value: dark contact cracks, darker edges and proper cast shadows. src/driftwood.py has one paint(...) call with every parameter you listed, and the five sheets cover all of them.

Remaining gaps, most visible first:

  • The gravel is a uniform speckle with no big light and dark areas; a blind viewer would notice this first.
  • Close up (30–40 px across), a log is a few wide flat bands of tone, and the grain still looks blocky.
  • Where piled logs cross, the joins are sometimes messy, and the logs are more alike in value than in the photo.
  • The root fans are too regular.

Numpy's default multithreading was making each cell take about 30 s; limiting it to one thread brought that down to 1–6 s and keeps within the 4-thread limit (src/run_all.sh sets this). I downloaded the extra photos with imgfetch.py as the brief asked; their sources are listed in refs/sources.tsv.

The student asks for native-resolution pixel art of lying logs by another artist, since Ferrari painted almost none. The next study they'd make is a copy of his near-ground passages, aimed at the gravel problem.

Everything is in ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/driftwood/. Start with LESSONS.md, which links every image; the sheets are in sheets/, and src/run_all.sh rebuilds it all in about 9 minutes.
